title: "Arab citizens of Israel"
---

# Arab citizens of Israel

| المواطنون العرب في إسرائيل עֲרָבִים אֶזרָחֵי יִשְׂרָאֵל |
| --- |
| Map of [Arab localities in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_localities_in_Israel), 2015 |
| Total population |
| [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_\(Israel\)), 2023: **2,065,000** (21%)[^cite_note-CBS2023-1][^cite_note-Arabs2013-2][East Jerusalem](343508.md) and [Golan Heights](7515865.md), 2012: **278,000** (\~3%) |
| Regions with significant populations |
| [State of Israel](9282173.md) |
| Languages |
| [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic)[^cite_note-3] and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Hebrew) |
| Religion |
| [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Israel) (84%)[^cite_note-4] [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel) (8%)[^cite_note-5] [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel) (8%)[^cite_note-CBS2019-6] |
| Related ethnic groups |
| Broader [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) groups such as [Palestinians](23267.md), [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and [Bedouins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouins) living beyond the borders of Israel. |

[Palestinian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabs) form the largest ethnic minority in [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-cfrel-7][^cite_note-8] Notions of identity among Israel's Arab citizens are complex, encompassing civic, religious, and ethnic components.[^cite_note-9] Most sources report that the majority of Arabs in Israel prefer to be identified as Palestinian citizens of Israel.[^cite_note-nyt2012-10][^cite_note-cfrel-7][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-PI-12][^cite_note-13]

In the wake of the [1948 Palestine war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war), the Israeli government [conferred Israeli citizenship upon all Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_citizenship_law#Status_of_Palestinian_Arabs) who had [remained or were not expelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_expulsion_and_flight). However, they were [subject to martial law until 1966](50477.md#Israel), while other Israeli citizens were not. In the early 1980s, Israel granted citizenship eligibility to the Palestinians in [East Jerusalem](343508.md) and the Syrian citizens of the [Golan Heights](7515865.md) by annexing both areas, though they remain internationally recognized as part of the [Israeli-occupied territories](2781576.md), which came into being after the [Six-Day War](29328.md) of 1967.[^cite_note-Question_of_Palestine:_Jerusalem-14] Acquisition of Israeli citizenship in East Jerusalem has been scarce, as only 5% of Palestinians in East Jerusalem were Israeli citizens in 2022, largely due to Palestinian society's disapproval of naturalization as [complicity with the occupation](46216.md). Israel has made the process more difficult, approving only 38% of new Palestinian applications from 2002 to 2022.[^cite_note-e-jer-cit-15]

According to the [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics), the Israeli Arab population stood at 2.1 million people in 2023, accounting for 21% of Israel's total population.[^cite_note-CBS2023-1] The majority of these Arab citizens identify themselves as Arab or Palestinian by nationality and as Israeli by citizenship.[^cite_note-ICG-16][^cite_note-Marcus-17][^cite_note-Ynetidentity-18] They mostly live in [Arab-majority towns and cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_localities_in_Israel), some of which are among the poorest in the country, and generally attend schools that are separated to some degree from those attended by [Jewish Israelis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Jews).[^cite_note-19] Arab political parties traditionally did not join governing coalitions until 2021, when the [United Arab List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_List) became the first to do so.[^cite_note-20] The [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel) and the [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Bedouin) in the [Negev](142273.md) and the [Galilee](12639.md) have historically expressed the strongest non-Jewish affinity to Israel and are more likely to identify as Israelis than other Arab citizens.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24]

Speakers of both [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), their traditional vernaculars are mostly [Levantine Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_Arabic) dialects, such as [Palestinian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabic) in central Israel and [Lebanese Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Arabic) further north, particularly among the [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Druze). [Negev Bedouins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Bedouins), on the other hand, speak a [Northwest Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Arabian_Arabic) dialect. Because the modern Arabic dialects of Israel's Arabs have absorbed multiple Hebrew loanwords and phrases, it is sometimes called the [Israeli Arabic dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language_in_Israel).[^cite_note-25] By religious affiliation, the majority of Arab Israelis are Muslims, but there are significant Christian and Druze minorities, among others.[^cite_note-26] Arab citizens of Israel have a wide variety of self-identification: as Israeli or "in Israel"; as Arabs or Palestinians; and as [Muslims](19541.md), [Christians](18337522.md) or [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze).[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2021-27]

## Terminology and identity

Main article: [Terms for Palestinian citizens of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terms_for_Palestinian_citizens_of_Israel)



Arabs in Israel, by natural region (2018).

The choice of terms to refer to Arab citizens of Israel is a highly politicized issue, and there is a broad range of labels that members of this community use to self-identity.[^cite_note-HRW-28][^cite_note-Lowrance-29] Generally speaking, supporters of Israel tend to use *Israeli Arab* or *Arab Israeli* to refer to this population without mentioning Palestine, while critics of Israel (or supporters of Palestinians) tend to use *Palestinian* or *Palestinian Arab* without referencing Israel.[^cite_note-Peleg-30] According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, most preferred to identify themselves as Palestinian citizens of Israel rather than as Israeli Arabs, as of 2012.[^cite_note-SI-31][^cite_note-32] *[The New York Times](30680.md)* uses both 'Palestinian Israelis'[^cite_note-33] and 'Israeli Arabs' to refer to the same population.



Israeli Arabs at a [Land Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Day) rally in [Sakhnin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhnin), 30 March 2010

The relationship of Arab citizens to the State of Israel is often fraught with tension and can be regarded in the context of relations between [minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group) populations and state authorities elsewhere in the world.[^cite_note-34] Arab citizens consider themselves to be an [indigenous people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples).[^cite_note-35]

### List of demonyms

Arab/Palestinian citizens of Israel may refer to themselves by a wide range of terms. Each of these names, while referring to the same group of people, connotes a different balance in what is often a multilayered identity assigning varying levels of priority or emphasis to the various dimensions which may be historic-geographic ("[Palestine (region)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\))"), "national" or ethnoreligious (Palestinian, Arab, Israeli, Druze, Circassian), linguistic (Arabic-speaking), civic (feeling "Israeli" or not), etc.:[^cite_note-icenter-36]

- **Palestinian citizens of Israel**

  [^cite_note-icenter-36]

  [^cite_note-cjr1-37]

  &#x20;is a term that most [Arab citizens of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Arabs) prefer to refer to themselves,

  [^cite_note-cfrel-7]

  [^cite_note-nyt2012-10]

  &#x20;and which some media ([BBC](19344654.md), *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)*, *[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post)*, [NBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC_News))

  [^cite_note-honest-38]

  &#x20;and other organizations use to refer to Israeli Arabs, either consistently or alternating the use of other terms for Israeli Arabs.

- Palestinian Arabs

  [^cite_note-cjr1-37]

- Palestinian Arabs in Israel

  [^cite_note-ICG-16]

  [^cite_note-Amara-39]

  [^cite_note-LDE-40]

- Palestinian Israelis

  [^cite_note-itfiai-41]

- Palestinians in Israel

  [^cite_note-cjr1-37]

- Israeli Arabs

  [^cite_note-itfiai-41]

  [^cite_note-cjr1-37]

  [^cite_note-icenter-36]

- Israeli Palestinian Arabs

  [^cite_note-ICG-16]

  [^cite_note-Amara-39]

  [^cite_note-LDE-40]

- Israeli Palestinians

  [^cite_note-icenter-36]

- Arab citizens of Israel

  [^cite_note-itfiai-41]

- Arab Israelis

  [^cite_note-itfiai-41]

- 48ers,

  [^cite_note-itfiai-41]

  [^cite_note-icenter-36]

  &#x20;'48 Arabs

  [^cite_note-cjr1-37]

Two appellations, among others listed above, are not applied to the [East Jerusalem](343508.md) Arab population or the [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) in the [Golan Heights](7515865.md), as these territories were occupied by Israel in 1967:

- *the Arabs inside the Green Line*

  [^cite_note-ICG-16]

  [^cite_note-Amara-39]

  [^cite_note-LDE-40]

- *the Arabs within* (Arabic: عرب الداخل, romanized: *‘Arab al-Dākhil*).

  [^cite_note-ICG-16]

  [^cite_note-Amara-39]

  [^cite_note-LDE-40]

### Demonym preferences

According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, as of 2012, most Israeli Arabs preferred to identify themselves as **Palestinian citizens of Israel** rather than as Israeli Arabs.[^cite_note-nyt2012-10] The *[Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations)* also states that most members of the Israeli Arab community prefer this term.[^cite_note-cfrel-7] *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* asserted in 2021 that "surveys showed" that Israeli Arabs preferred the term "Palestinian citizen of Israel" and that "for people who often feel caught between two worlds, however, the contours of what it means to be a Palestinian citizen of Israel remain a work in progress."[^cite_note-PI-12] However, these findings conflict with a 2017 [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University) poll which showed most Israelis self-identify as either Arab-Israeli or simply Israeli.[^cite_note-2017poll-42]

Similar terms that Israeli Arabs, media and other organizations may use are *Palestinian Arabs in Israel* and *Israeli Palestinian Arabs*. Amnesty reports that "Arab citizens of Israel" is "an inclusive term used by Israel that describes a number of different and primarily Arabic-speaking groups, including Muslim Arabs", Christian Arabs, Druze and Circassians. They further stated that "considering the number of those defined as Muslim Arabs and Christian Arabs together, the population of Palestinian citizens of Israel amounted to around 1.8 million" in 2019.[^cite_note-43]

There are at least two terms which specifically exclude the [East Jerusalem](343508.md) Arab population and the [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and other Arabs in the [Golan Heights](7515865.md): *the Arabs inside the Green Line*, and *the Arabs within* (Arabic: عرب الداخل, romanized: *‘Arab al-Dākhil*).[^cite_note-ICG-16][^cite_note-Amara-39][^cite_note-LDE-40] These terms clarify that

- Although Israel annexed [East Jerusalem](343508.md) in 1967, the vast majority of its Arab population does not have Israeli citizenship

- Although Israel annexed the [Golan Heights](7515865.md), that area was originally part of [Syria](7515849.md), not [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine).

#### Identification as Palestinian

While known officially by the Israeli government only as "Israeli Arabs" or "Arab Israelis", the development of Palestinian nationalism and identity in the 20th and 21st centuries has been met by a marked evolution in self-identification, reflecting a rising identification with Palestinian identity alongside Arab and Israeli signifiers.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-ICG-16][^cite_note-Ynetidentity-18] A number of Palestinian citizens of Israel have family ties to Palestinians in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md) as well as to [Palestinian refugees](74681.md) in [Jordan](7515964.md), [Syria](7515849.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md).[^cite_note-TuckerRoberts2008-45]

Between 1948 and 1967, few Arab citizens of Israel identified openly as "Palestinian", and an "Israeli-Arab" identity, the preferred phrase of the Israeli establishment and public, was predominant.[^cite_note-Peleg-30] Public expressions of Palestinian identity, such as displays of the [Palestinian flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_flag) or the singing and reciting of nationalist songs or poetry were illegal.[^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46] With the end of military administrative rule in 1966 and following the 1967 war, national consciousness and its expression among Israel's Arab citizens spread.[^cite_note-Peleg-30][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46] A majority then self-identified as Palestinian, preferring this descriptor to Israeli Arab in numerous surveys over the years.[^cite_note-Peleg-30][^cite_note-Waxmanp12-47][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46] In a 2017 telephone poll, 40% of Arab citizens of Israel identified as "Arab in Israel / Arab citizen of Israel", 15% identified as "Palestinian", 8.9% as "Palestinian in Israel / Palestinian citizen of Israel", and 8.7% as "Arab";[^cite_note-2017poll-42][^cite_note-PV-48] the focus groups associated with the poll provided a different outcome, in which "there was consensus that Palestinian identity occupies a central place in their consciousness".[^cite_note-2017poll-42] A November 2023 poll asked respondents from this demographic what the most important "component in their personal identity" was to them; 33 percent answered "Israeli citizenship", 32 percent "Arab identity", 23 percent "religious affiliation", and 8 percent "Palestinian identity".[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51]

University of Haifa professor [Sammy Smooha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Smooha) commented in 2019, "The largest now and the most growing identity is a hybrid identity, which is 'Palestinian in Israel' or a similar combination. I think that’s what’s going to take over."[^cite_note-foreignpolicy-52]

##### Distinction of Druze and Circassian citizens

In the [Amnesty International](18947898.md) 2022 report "Israel's Apartheid against Palestinians: Cruel System of Domination and Crime against Humanity", the organization excludes the Israeli Arab Druze and non-Arab [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Israel) from the term Palestinian Arab citizens of Israel:

- The [Israeli Ministry of Foreign Affairs](18133296.md) officially classifies the roughly 2.1 million Palestinian citizens of Israel as "Arab citizens of Israel", reflecting their attributing a racialized non-Jewish, Arab status to all of them

- The term "Arab citizens of Israel" includes Muslim Arabs including [Bedouins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouins_in_Israel), Christian Arabs, the 20–25,000 [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel), and even the 4–5,000 [Circassians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians_in_Israel), whose origins are in the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus) but are mostly Muslim.

- According to Amnesty, the Israeli state views and treats Palestinian citizens of Israel differently from the Druze and Circassians, who must for example serve in the army while Palestinian citizens need not serve.

- Nonetheless, Israeli authorities and media refer to those who self-identify as Palestinians – as "Israeli Arabs".

The *Washington Post* included the Druze among the Palestinians.[^cite_note-PI-12][^cite_note-53] The Council of Foreign Relations stated:"The majority of Arab citizens are Sunni Muslims, though there are many Christians and also Druze, who more often embrace Israeli identity."[^cite_note-cfrel-7]

#### Identification as Arab Israeli

The question of Palestinian identity extends to representation in the Israeli [Knesset](74656.md). Journalist Ruth Margalit says of [Mansour Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Abbas) of the [United Arab List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_List), a member of the [governing coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty-sixth_government_of_Israel), "The traditional term for this group, Arab Israelis, is increasingly controversial, but it's the one that Abbas prefers."[^cite_note-54] Abbas gave an interview to Israeli media in November 2021 and said "My rights don't just come from my citizenship. My rights also come from being a member of the Palestinian people, a son of this Palestinian homeland. And whether we like it or not, the State of Israel, with its identity, was established inside the Palestinian homeland."[^cite_note-55] [Sami Abu Shehadeh](61848927.md) of [Balad](44494596.md) is "an outspoken advocate of Palestinian identity".[^cite_note-PI-12][^cite_note-PQ-56] He says, referring to the [2021 Israel–Palestine crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis), "... If the past weeks provided lessons for the international community, then a main one is that they cannot continue to ignore the Palestinian citizens of Israel. Any solution should include full equality for all citizens as well as the respect and recognition of our rights as a national minority."[^cite_note-57]

### Israeli surveys

Surveys of Arab-Israeli self-identification are diverse, and have often presented differing if not contradictory results. In 2017 the [Konrad Adenauer Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Adenauer_Foundation)'s Program for Jewish-Arab Cooperation at the [Moshe Dayan Center for Middle Eastern and African Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moshe_Dayan_Center_for_Middle_Eastern_and_African_Studies) at [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University) conducted a telephone poll, in which the results were:[^cite_note-2017poll-42][^cite_note-PV-48]

- National identity with Israeli civil component 49.7%, of which&#x20;

  - Palestinian (citizen) of Israel 8.9%

  - Arab (citizen) of Israel 40.8%

- Pure national identity 24.1%, of which&#x20;

  - Palestinian 15.4%

  - Arab 8.7%

- Civil identity: Israeli 11.4%

- Religious identity 9.5%

- Other / Don't know 5.3%

The focus groups associated with the poll provided a different outcome, in which "there was consensus that Palestinian identity occupies a central place in their consciousness". reflecting "the strength of Palestinian-Arab identity", and that they do not see a contradiction between that and Israeli *civic* identity. The focus group revealed strong opposition to the term "Israeli-Arab" and to the concept of Israel's "Independence Day". The study concluded that the focus group findings of strong Palestinian national identity, not conflicting with Israeli civic identity, match those seen in the public sphere.[^cite_note-2017poll-42]

According to a 2019 survey by [University of Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Haifa) professor [Sammy Smooha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sammy_Smooha), conducted in Arabic among 718 Arab adults, 47% of the Arab population chose Palestinian identities with an Israeli component ("Israeli Palestinian", "Palestinian in Israel", "Palestinian Arab in Israel"), 36% prefers Israeli Arab identities without a Palestinian component ("Israeli", "Arab", "Arab in Israel", "Israeli Arab"), and 15% chose Palestinian identities without an Israeli component ("Palestinian", "Palestinian Arab"). When these two components are presented as competitors, 69% chose exclusive or primary Palestinian identity, compared with 30% who chose exclusive or primary Israeli Arab identity. 66% of the Arab population agreed that "the identity of 'Palestinian Arab in Israel' is appropriate to most Arabs in Israel."[^cite_note-Smooha_2019-58]

According to a 2020 survey by [Camil Fuchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camil_Fuchs?action=edit\&redlink=1) of [Tel-Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel-Aviv_University), 51% of Arabs identify as Arab-Israeli, 7% identify as Palestinian, 23% identify as Israeli, 15% identify as Arab, and 4% identify as "other." This significantly differs from their 2019 survey, in which 49% identified as Arab-Israeli, 18% as Palestinian, 27% as Arab, and 5% as Israeli.[^cite_note-59]

### Academic practice

Common practice in contemporary academic literature is to identify this community as *Palestinian* as it is how the majority self-identify (See [Self-Identification](#Self-identification) for more).[^cite_note-Waxmanp12-47] Terms preferred by most Arab citizens to identify themselves include *Palestinians*, *Palestinians in Israel*, *Israeli Palestinians*, *the Palestinians of 1948*, *Palestinian Arabs*, *Palestinian Arab citizens of Israel* or *Palestinian citizens of Israel*.[^cite_note-ICG-16][^cite_note-HRW-28][^cite_note-Lowrance-29][^cite_note-Amara-39][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46][^cite_note-Muwasi-60] There are, however, individuals from among the Arab citizenry who reject the term *Palestinian* altogether.[^cite_note-HRW-28] A minority of Israel's Arab citizens include "Israeli" in some way in their self-identifying label; the majority identify as [Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people) by nationality and Israeli by [citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship).[^cite_note-Marcus-17][^cite_note-Lowrance-29]

### Israeli establishment

The Israeli establishment prefers *Israeli Arabs* or *Arabs in Israel*, and also uses the terms *the minorities*, *the Arab sector*, *Arabs of Israel* and *Arab citizens of Israel*.[^cite_note-ICG-16][^cite_note-Amara-39][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46][^cite_note-LDE-40][^cite_note-Rabinowitzp43-61] These labels have been criticized for denying this population a political or national identification, obscuring their Palestinian identity and connection to [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)).[^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46][^cite_note-LDE-40][^cite_note-Rabinowitzp43-61] The term *Israeli Arabs* in particular is viewed as a construct of the Israeli authorities.[^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46][^cite_note-LDE-40][^cite_note-Rabinowitzp43-61][^cite_note-Jamal-62] It is nonetheless used by a significant minority of the Arab population, "reflecting its dominance in Israeli social discourse."[^cite_note-Lowrance-29]

### Historical

Between 1920 and 1948, in what was then [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), all citizens were known as Palestinians, and the two primary communities were referred to by the British authorities as "Arabs" and "[Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Jews)". Between [1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestine_war) and [1967](29328.md), few citizens of Israel identified openly as "Palestinian". An "Israeli-Arab" identity, the preferred phrase of the Israeli establishment and public, was predominant.[^cite_note-Peleg-30] Public expressions of Palestinian identity, such as displays of the [Palestinian flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_flag) or the singing and reciting of nationalist songs or poetry were illegal.[^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46] Ever since the 1948 [Nakba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakba), the Palestinians that have remained within the [1949 Armistice borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Armistice_borders) have been colloquially known as "48 Arabs" (Arabic: عرب ٤٨, romanized: *ʿArab Thamāniya wa-ʾArbaʿīn*).[^cite_note-PI-12][^cite_note-PQ-56] With the end of military administrative rule in 1966 and following the 1967 war, national consciousness and its expression among Israel's Arab citizens spread.[^cite_note-Peleg-30][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46] A majority then self-identified as Palestinian, preferring this descriptor to Israeli Arab in numerous surveys over the years.[^cite_note-Peleg-30][^cite_note-Waxmanp12-47][^cite_note-Torstrickp13-46]

### East Jerusalem and Golan Heights

Arabs in East Jerusalem and the Golan Heights (Syrian Golan) are special cases regarding citizenship and identity.

Arabs living in [East Jerusalem](343508.md), occupied and administered by Israel since the Six-Day War of 1967 hold Israeli ID cards, but most are non-citizen permanent residents since few accepted Israel's offer of citizenship after the war's end, refusing to recognize its sovereignty, and most maintain close ties with the West Bank.[^cite_note-BBCEJ-63] As permanent residents, they are eligible to vote in [Jerusalem](16043.md)'s municipal elections, although only a small percentage takes advantage of this right.

The Golan Heights was not part of [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) or the Ottoman political units which preceded it, but rather was part of Syria, and the UN still recognizes it as such, and calls it the Syrian Golan.[^cite_note-64] The remaining [Druze population of the Golan Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel#Status_and_position_of_Golan_Heights_Druze), occupied and administered by Israel in 1967, are considered permanent residents under Israel's [Golan Heights Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Heights_Law) of 1981. As of mid-2022, 4,303 Druze citizens of [Syria](7515849.md) have been granted Israeli citizenship, or, 20% of the total Druze residents in the Golan Heights.[^cite_note-65] In 2024, [Rami Zeedan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rami_Zeedan) estimated that approximately 25% have Israeli citizenship.[^cite_note-66]

## History

### 1948 Arab–Israeli War

Most Jewish Israelis refer to the [1948 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_War) as the War of Independence, while most Arab citizens refer to it as [al-Nakba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_exodus) (the catastrophe), a reflection of differences in perception of the purpose and outcomes of the war.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-Masalhap221-68]

In the aftermath of the 1947–49 war, the territory previously administered by the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) as [Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine) was de facto divided into three parts: the State of Israel, the [Jordanian](7515964.md)-held West Bank, and the [Egyptian](8087628.md)-held Gaza Strip. Of the estimated 950,000 Arabs that lived in the territory that became Israel before the war,[^cite_note-al-Jazeera-20031209-69] over 80% fled or were expelled. The other 20%, some 156,000, remained.[^cite_note-70] Some of them supported Israel from the beginning.[^cite_note-71] Arab citizens of Israel today are largely composed of the people who remained and their descendants. Others include some from the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) and the [West Bank](33209.md) who procured Israeli citizenship under family-unification provisions made significantly more stringent in the aftermath of the [Second Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Intifada).[^cite_note-72]

Arabs who left their homes during the period of armed conflict, but remained in what had become Israeli territory, were considered to be "[present absentees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Present_absentee)". In some cases, they were refused permission to return to their homes, which were [expropriated](144089.md) and turned over to state ownership, as was the property of other [Palestinian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_refugee).[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] Some 274,000, or 1 of every 4 Arab citizens of Israel are "present absentees" or [internally displaced Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_Displaced_Palestinians).[^cite_note-Badil-75][^cite_note-IDMC-76] Notable cases of "present absentees" include the residents of [Saffuriyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepphoris) and the [Galilee](12639.md) villages of [Kafr Bir'im](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Bir'im) and [Iqrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iqrit).[^cite_note-77]

### 1949–1966



[Seif el-Din el-Zubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seif_el-Din_el-Zubi), member of the first Knesset

Between [Israel's declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Declaration_of_Independence) on 14 May 1948 and the Israeli Nationality Law of 14 July 1952, there technically were no Israeli citizens.[^cite_note-78]

While most Arabs remaining in Israel were granted citizenship, they were subject to martial law in the early years of the state.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) had given little serious thought as to how to integrate Arabs, and according to [Ian Lustick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Lustick) subsequent policies were 'implemented by a rigorous regime of military rule that dominated what remained of the Arab population in territory ruled by Israel, enabling the state to expropriate most Arab-owned land, severely limit its access to investment capital and employment opportunity, and eliminate virtually all opportunities to use citizenship as a vehicle for gaining political influence'.[^cite_note-81] A variety of Israeli legislative measures facilitated the transfer of land abandoned by Arabs to state ownership. These included the [Absentee Property Law of 1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_and_Property_Laws_in_Israel#The_'Absentees_Property_Law') which allowed the state to expropriate the property of Palestinians who fled or were expelled to other countries, and the Land Acquisition Law of 1953 which authorized the Ministry of Finance to transfer expropriated land to the state. Other common legal expedients included the use of emergency regulations to declare land belonging to Arab citizens a closed military zone, followed by the use of Ottoman legislation on abandoned land to take control of the land.[^cite_note-82] Travel permits, curfews, [administrative detentions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_detention), and expulsions were part of life until 1966.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84]

Arabs who held Israeli citizenship were entitled to vote for the Israeli [Knesset](74656.md). Arab Knesset members have served in office since the [First Knesset in 1949](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_Israeli_legislative_election). The first Arab Knesset members were [Amin-Salim Jarjora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amin-Salim_Jarjora) and [Seif el-Din el-Zoubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seif_el-Din_el-Zoubi) who were members of the [Democratic List of Nazareth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_List_of_Nazareth) party and [Tawfik Toubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfik_Toubi), member of the [Maki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maki_\(current_political_party\)) party.

In 1965 a radical independent Arab group called *al-Ard* forming the Arab Socialist List tried to run for [Knesset](74656.md) elections. The list was banned by the [Israeli Central Elections Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Central_Elections_Committee).[^cite_note-85]

In 1966, martial law was lifted completely, and the government set about dismantling most of the discriminatory laws, while Arab citizens were granted the same rights as Jewish citizens under law.[^cite_note-86]

### 1967–2000



A monument to residents of [Arraba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arraba,_Galilee) killed in the Arab–Israeli conflict

After the 1967 [Six-Day War](29328.md), Arab citizens were able to contact Palestinians in the [West Bank](33209.md) and [Gaza Strip](12047.md) for the first time since the establishment of the state. This, along with the lifting of military rule, led to increased political activism among Arab citizens.[^cite_note-Identity_Crisis-87][^cite_note-88]

In 1974, a committee of Arab mayors and municipal councilmen was established which played an important role in representing the community and pressuring the Israeli government.[^cite_note-89] This was followed in 1975 by the formation of the Committee for the Defense of the Land, which sought to prevent continuing land expropriations.[^cite_note-90] That same year, a political breakthrough took place with the election of Arab poet [Tawfiq Ziad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfiq_Ziad), a [Maki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maki_\(current_political_party\)) member, as mayor of Nazareth, accompanied by a strong communist presence in the town council.[^cite_note-91] In 1976, six Arab citizens of Israel were killed by Israeli security forces at a protest against land expropriations and house demolitions. The date of the protest, 30 March, has since been commemorated annually as [Land Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Day).

The 1980s saw the birth of the [Islamic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Movement_in_Israel). As part of a larger trend in the [Arab World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World), the movement emphasized moving Islam into the political realm. They built schools, provided other essential social services, constructed mosques, and encouraged prayer and conservative Islamic dress. The Islamic Movement began to affect electoral politics particularly at the local level.[^cite_note-struggle-92]

Many Arab citizens supported the [First Intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Intifada) and assisted Palestinians in the West Bank and Gaza, providing them with money, food, and clothes. A number of strikes were also held by Arab citizens in solidarity with Palestinians in the occupied territories.[^cite_note-struggle-92]

The years leading up to the [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords) were a time of optimism for Arab citizens. During the administration of [Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin), Arab parties played an important role in the formation of a governing coalition. Increased participation of Arab citizens was also seen at the civil society level. However, tension continued to exist with multiple Arabs calling for Israel to become a "[state of all its citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalism)", thereby challenging the state's Jewish identity. In the 1999 elections for prime minister, 94% of the Arab electorate voted for [Ehud Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Barak). However, Barak formed a broad left-right-center government without consulting the Arab parties, disappointing the Arab community.[^cite_note-Identity_Crisis-87]

### 2000–present



Arab Israelis from Shefa-'Amr demonstrating in front of the Haifa court building with Palestinian flags

Tensions between Arabs and the state rose in [October 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2000_events) when 12 Arab citizens and one man from Gaza were killed while protesting the government's response to the [Second Intifada](71717.md). In response to this incident, the government established the [Or Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Or_Commission). The events of October 2000 caused multiple Arabs to question the nature of their Israeli citizenship. To a large extent, they boycotted the [2001 Israeli Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Israeli_prime_ministerial_election) as a means of protest.[^cite_note-Identity_Crisis-87] This boycott helped Ariel Sharon defeat Ehud Barak; as aforementioned, in the 1999 elections, 94 percent of Israel's Arab minority had voted for Ehud Barak.[^cite_note-93] [IDF](39237.md) enlistment by [Bedouin](61770.md) citizens of Israel dropped significantly.[^cite_note-94]

During the [2006 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War), Arab advocacy organizations complained that the Israeli government had invested time and effort to protect Jewish citizens from Hezbollah attacks, but had neglected Arab citizens. They pointed to a dearth of bomb shelters in Arab towns and villages and a lack of basic emergency information in Arabic.[^cite_note-Arab_Citizens_and_the_2006_War-95] Multiple Israeli Jews viewed the Arab opposition to government policy and sympathy with the Lebanese as a sign of disloyalty.[^cite_note-Arabs_and_War_in_North-96]

In October 2006, tensions rose when Israeli Prime Minister [Ehud Olmert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Olmert) invited a right-wing political party [Yisrael Beiteinu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yisrael_Beiteinu), to join his coalition government. The party leader, [Avigdor Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avigdor_Lieberman), advocated an ethnicity-based territory exchange, the [Lieberman Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieberman_Plan), by transferring heavily populated Arab areas (mainly [the Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_\(Israel\))), to Palestinian Authority control and annexing major Jewish Israeli settlement blocs in the West Bank close to the green line as part of a peace proposal.[^cite_note-VOAnews-20061024-97] Arabs who would prefer to remain in Israel instead of becoming citizens of a Palestinian state would be able to move to Israel. All citizens of Israel, whether Jews or Arabs, would be required to pledge an oath of allegiance to retain citizenship. Those who refuse could remain in Israel as permanent residents.[^cite_note-Timothy_Waters-98]

In January 2007 the first non-Druze Arab minister in Israel's history, [Raleb Majadele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raleb_Majadele), was appointed [minister without portfolio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_without_portfolio#Israel) ([Salah Tarif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salah_Tarif), a [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), had been appointed a minister without portfolio in 2001). The appointment was criticized by the left, which felt it was an attempt to cover up the Labor Party's decision to sit with Yisrael Beiteinu in the government, and by the right, who saw it as a threat to Israel's status as a [Jewish state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_state).[^cite_note-Stern-Raleb-99][^cite_note-Haaretz-20070112-100] In 2021, [Mansour Abbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansour_Abbas), the leader of the [United Arab List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_List), made history by becoming the first Israeli Arab political leader to join an [Israeli governing coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty-sixth_government_of_Israel).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102]

During the [2021 Israel–Palestine crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis) widespread protests and riots intensified across Israel, particularly in cities with large Arab populations. In Lod, rocks were thrown at Jewish apartments and some Jewish residents were evacuated from their homes by the police. Synagogues and a Muslim cemetery were vandalized.[^cite_note-103] Communal violence including "riots, stabbings, arson, attempted home invasions and shootings" was reported from Beersheba, Rahat, Ramla, Lod, Nasiriyah, Tiberias, Jerusalem, Haifa and Acre.[^cite_note-104]



A protest against the killings in the Arab community in [Tel Aviv](31453.md)

The Arab community in Israel has seen a significant increase in [violence and organized crime](15556664.md), including a rise in gang-related murders in recent years.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] A report by the [Abraham Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Initiative?action=edit\&redlink=1) highlighted that 244 Arab community members were killed in Israel in 2023, more than double the previous year's count.[^cite_note-DT-107][^cite_note-108] The report attributed this surge in homicides directly to National Security Minister [Itamar Ben Gvir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itamar_Ben_Gvir), who campaigned on a platform promising to improve personal security and oversees law enforcement.[^cite_note-DT-107] Prominent [organized crime families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_mafia) among Israeli Arabs include Al-Hariri, Bakri, Jarushis, and the Druze Abu Latifs.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]

Since the outbreak of the [Gaza war](74998519.md), Israel has carried out [mass arrests and detentions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_detentions_in_the_2023_Israel%E2%80%93Hamas_war) of Palestinian workers and Arab citizens of Israel.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] On 5 November 2023, [CNN](62028.md) reported that "dozens" of Palestinian residents and Arab Israelis were arrested in Israel for expressions of solidarity with the civilian population of Gaza, sharing [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) verses, or expressing "any support for the Palestinian people".[^cite_note-114] *[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz)* described the widespread targeting of Arab Israelis by Israeli security forces.[^cite_note-115] Referring to "hundreds" of interrogations, *[El País](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Pa%C3%ADs)* reported on 11 November that Israel increasingly treats its Arab minority as a "potential [fifth column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_column)".[^cite_note-116] At the same time, the war saw an increased self-identification with Israel among Arab citizens[^cite_note-117] and continued cooperation with their Jewish counterparts in various areas, including healthcare, military service, education and sports.[^cite_note-118] According to different polls, a majority of Israeli Arabs condemned the [7 October attacks](74998317.md), but also opposed the mass bombardment of Gaza. Multiple Israeli Arabs expressed a general resentment over the war, as other Palestinians regarded them as supporters of Israel, whereas Israeli Jews saw them as potential [Hamas](13913.md) supporters.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

## Sectarian and religious groupings

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Muslim |  | 82% |
| Christian |  | 9% |
| Druze |  | 9% |

In 2006, the official number of Arab residents in Israel – including East Jerusalem and Golan Heights permanent residents some of whom are not citizens – was 1,413,500 people, about 20% of Israel's population.[^cite_note-Ynet-121] The Arab population in 2023 was estimated at 2,065,000, representing 21% of the country's population.[^cite_note-CBS2023-1][^cite_note-Arabs2013-2] According to the [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) (May 2003), Muslims, including Bedouins, make up 82% of the entire Arab population in Israel, along with around 9% Druze, and 9% Christians.[^cite_note-Statistics-122] Projections based on 2010 data, predicted that Arab Israelis would constitute 25% of Israel's population by 2025.[^cite_note-123]

The national language and mother tongue of Arab citizens, including the Druze, is [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) and the colloquial spoken language is of the [Palestinian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabic) dialect. Knowledge and command of [Modern Standard Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic) varies.[^cite_note-Spolsky-124]

### Muslims

Main article: [Islam in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Israel)



Muslim performs prayers in [El-Jazzar Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El-Jazzar_Mosque).

Muslims comprise 17.9% of the Israeli population in 2019.[^cite_note-Muslim2020-125] The majority of Muslims in Israel are [Sunni Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Arabs),[^cite_note-Pew1-126] with an [Ahmadiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya) minority.[^cite_note-127] There are around 4,000 [Alawites](436616.md) in Israel and the majority of them live in [Ghajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghajar) village in the occupied [Golan Heights](7515865.md) near the border with [Lebanon](17771.md). The [Bedouin in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouin_in_Israel) are also Arab Muslims, with some Bedouin clans participating in the [Israeli army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_army). The small [Circassian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circassians) community is composed of Sunni Muslims uprooted from the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus) in the late 19th century. In addition, smaller populations of [Kurdish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Israel), [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dom_people) and [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turks_in_Israel) Muslims also live in Israel.

In 2020, [Jerusalem](16043.md) hosted the largest Muslim population in Israel, numbering 346,000 residents, constituting 21.1% of Israel's Muslim population and about 36.9% of the city's total residents. [Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahat) followed with the second-largest Muslim population at 71,300 residents, while [Umm Al-Fahm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Al-Fahm) and [Nazareth](21702.md) had approximately 56,000 and 55,600 residents, respectively.[^cite_note-Muslim2020-125] The eleven towns of the [Triangle area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_\(Israel\)) are home to approximately 250,000 Israeli Muslims.[^cite_note-128]

Regarding regional distribution in 2020, approximately 35.2% of Israeli Muslims resided in the [Northern District](324281.md), 21.9% in the [Jerusalem District](844333.md), 17.1% in the [Central District](844282.md), 13.7% in the [Haifa District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_District), 10.9% in the [Southern District](844338.md), and 1.2% in the [Tel Aviv District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_District).[^cite_note-Muslim2020-125] The Israeli Muslim population is notably youthful: about 33.4% are aged 14 and under, while those aged 65 and older account for only 4.3%. Furthermore, the Muslim community in Israel boasts the highest [fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertility_rate) among religious groups, standing at 3.16 children per woman.[^cite_note-Muslim2020-125]



Worshipers in one of the mosques in [Umm al-Fahm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_al-Fahm)

According to study published by [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center) in 2016, while Muslims living in Israel, overall, are more religious than Israeli Jews, they are less religious than Muslims living in other countries in the Middle East. Muslim women are more likely to say that religion has high importance in their lives, and younger Muslims are generally less observant than their elders.[^cite_note-Pew1-126] According to the [Israel Democracy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Democracy_Institute) survey conducted in 2015, 47% of Israeli Muslims identified as traditional, 32% identified as religious, 17% identified as not religious at all, 3% identified as very religious.[^cite_note-Index_Democracy_Israeli-129]

#### Settled

Traditionally settled communities of [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) Arabs comprise about 70% of the Arab population in Israel. In 2010, the average number of children per mother was 3.84, dropping from 3.97 in 2008. The Muslim population is mostly young: 42% of Muslims are under the age of 15. The median age of Muslim Israelis is 18, while the median age of Jewish Israelis is 30. The percentage of people over 65 is less than 3% for Muslims, compared with 12% for the Jewish population.[^cite_note-Statistics-122]

#### Bedouin

See also: [Israeli Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Bedouin)



[Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahat), the largest Bedouin city in the Negev

According to the [Foreign Affairs Minister of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Affairs_Minister_of_Israel), 110,000 Bedouins live in the [Negev](142273.md), 50,000 in the [Galilee](12639.md), and 10,000 in the central region of Israel.[^cite_note-Bedouin-130]

Prior to the establishment of Israel in 1948, there were an estimated 65,000–90,000 Bedouin living in the Negev.[^cite_note-Bedouin-130] The 11,000 who remained were relocated by the Israeli government in the 1950s and 1960s to an area in the northeastern Negev comprising 10% of the Negev desert.[^cite_note-Bedouin-130] The Israeli government built seven development towns for the Bedouin between 1979 and 1982. Around half the Bedouin population live in these towns, the largest of which is the city of [Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahat), others being [Ar'arat an-Naqab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ar'arat_an-Naqab) (Ar'ara BaNegev), [Bir Hadaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bir_Hadaj), [Hura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hura), [Kuseife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuseife), [Lakiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakiya), [Shaqib al-Salam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaqib_al-Salam) (Segev Shalom) and [Tel as-Sabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_as-Sabi) (Tel Sheva). In 2005, Bedouin were estimated to amount to 10% of the Arab citizens of Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbu-Sa‘ad2005113–114-131] Approximately 40–50% of Bedouin citizens of Israel live in 39–45 [unrecognized villages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrecognized_Bedouin_villages_in_Israel) that are not connected to the electrical grid and water mains.[^cite_note-hrw\.org-132][^cite_note-133] A study published by the Taub Center for Social Policy Studies in 2017 found that Bedouins have the lowest achievements in the Arab sector on all indices: [bagrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrut) scores, rates of college graduates, and fields of employment. As they tend to be the least educated.[^cite_note-AI-134]<sup>: 42 </sup>

### Druze

Main articles: [Druze in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel) and [Druze in Mandatory Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Mandatory_Palestine)



[Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Druze) dignitaries celebrating the [Ziyarat al-Nabi Shu'ayb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziyarat_al-Nabi_Shu'ayb) festival at the tomb of the prophet in [Hittin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittin)

Most Israeli [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) reside in the northern part of the country and are officially recognized as a separate religious community with their own courts.[^cite_note-135] They maintain [Arabic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_culture) as integral parts of their identity, and Arabic is their primary language.[^cite_note-Brockman_2011_259-136] The Galilean Druze and Druze of the [Haifa](55606.md) region received Israeli citizenship automatically in 1948. After Israel captured the [Golan Heights](7515865.md) from Syria in 1967 and annexed it to Israel in 1981, the [Druze of the Golan Heights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel#Status_and_position_of_Golan_Heights_Druze) were offered full Israeli citizenship under the [Golan Heights Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golan_Heights_Law). Most declined Israeli citizenship and retain [Syrian](7515849.md) citizenship and identity and are treated as permanent residents of Israel.[^cite_note-Scott_Wilson-137] As of 2011, fewer than 10% of the Druze population in the Golan Heights had accepted Israeli citizenship.[^cite_note-138]

At the end of 2019, approximately 81% of the Israeli Druze population lived in the [Northern District](324281.md) and 19% lived in the [Haifa District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_District), and the largest population of Druze were [Daliyat al-Karmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daliyat_al-Karmel) and [Yirka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yirka). Israeli Druze live in 19 towns and villages, either singly or mixed with Christians and Muslims, all located on the tops of the mountains in northern Israel ([Upper](8232648.md) and [Lower Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Galilee) and [Mount Carmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Carmel)), including [Abu Snan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Snan), [Beit Jann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Jann), [Daliyat al-Karmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daliyat_al-Karmel), [Ein al-Asad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_al-Asad), [Hurfeish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurfeish), [Isfiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfiya), [Julis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julis), [Kafr Yasif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Yasif), [Kisra-Sumei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisra-Sumei), [Maghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghar,_Israel), [Peki'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peki'in), [Rameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rameh), [Sajur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajur), [Shefa-Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shefa-Amr), [Yanuh-Jat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yanuh-Jat), and [Yarka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarka).[^cite_note-DP-139] There are four remaining Druze villages in the Israeli-annexed portion of the [Golan Heights](7515865.md)—[Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams), [Mas'ade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mas'ade), [Buq'ata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buq'ata), and [Ein Qiniyye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_Qiniyye)—in which 23,000 Druze live.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142]

During the [British Mandate for Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), the Druze did not embrace the rising [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) of the time or participate in violent confrontations. In 1948, multiple Druze volunteered for the Israeli army and no Druze villages were destroyed or permanently abandoned.[^cite_note-IDMC-76] Since the establishment of the state, the Druze have demonstrated solidarity with Israel and distanced themselves from Arab and Islamic radicalism.[^cite_note-143] Male Druze citizens serve in the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md).[^cite_note-Christian_Arabs-144]

From 1957, the Israeli government formally recognized the Druze as a separate religious community,[^cite_note-Jiryis-1969-145][^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] and are defined as a distinct ethnic group in the [Israeli Ministry of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(Israel\))'s census registration. On the other hand, the [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) categorizes Druze as Arabs in their census.[^cite_note-148] While the Israeli education system is basically divided into Hebrew and Arabic speaking schools, the Druze have autonomy within the Arabic speaking branch.[^cite_note-Jiryis-1969-145] Israeli Druze are Arabic in language and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_culture),[^cite_note-Brockman_2011_259-136] and their mother tongue is the [Arabic Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_Language).

Some scholars maintain that Israel has tried to separate the Druze from other Arab communities, and that the effort has influenced the way Israel's Druze perceive their modern identity.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] Survey data suggests that Israeli Druze prioritize their identity first as Druze (religiously), second as Arabs (culturally and ethnically), and third as Israelis (citizenship-wise).[^cite_note-Nili2019-151] A small minority of them identify as [Palestinians](23267.md), distinguishing them from the majority of other Arab citizens of Israel, who predominantly identify as Palestinians.[^cite_note-Nili2019-151]

In a survey conducted in 2008 by Dr. Yusuf Hassan of [Tel Aviv University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University) 94% of Druze respondents identified as "Druze-Israelis" in the religious and national context,[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] while a 2017 Pew Research Center poll reported that while 99% of Muslims and 96% of Christians identified as ethnically Arab, a smaller share of Druze, 71%, identified likewise.[^cite_note-154] Compared to other Christians and Muslims, Druze place less emphasis on their [Arab identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity) and self-identify more as Israeli. Most do not identify as [Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people).[^cite_note-AmaraSchnell-155] However, they were less ready for personal relationships with [Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Jews) compared to Israeli Muslims and Christians.[^cite_note-Index_Democracy_Israeli-129] Scholars attribute this trend to cultural differences between Jews and Druze.[^cite_note-156] Druze politicians in Israel include [Ayoob Kara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayoob_Kara), who represented [Likud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likud) in the [Knesset](74656.md); [Majalli Wahabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majalli_Wahabi) of [Kadima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kadima), the Deputy Speaker of the Knesset; and [Said Nafa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Nafa) of the Arab party [Balad](44494596.md).[^cite_note-157]

### Christians

Main article: [Christianity in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel)

See also: [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) and [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians)



[Catholic Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Mass) in the [Basilica of the Annunciation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_Annunciation) in [Nazareth](21702.md), [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) are one of the most educated ethnoreligious groups in Israel.

[^cite_note-Christians_in_Israel:_Strong_in_education-158]

Christian Arabs comprise about 9% of the Arab population in Israel. At the end of 2019, approximately 70.6% reside in the [Northern District](324281.md), 13.3% in the [Haifa District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_District), 9.5% in the [Jerusalem District](844333.md), 3.4% in the [Central District](844282.md), 2.7% in the [Tel Aviv District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_District) and 0.5% in the [Southern District](844338.md).[^cite_note-Christian2020-159] There are 135,000 or more Christian Arabs in Israel (and more than 39,000 non-Arab Christians).[^cite_note-Christian2020-159][^cite_note-160] As of 2014 the [Melkite Greek Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melkite_Greek_Catholic_Church) was the largest Christian community in Israel, where about 60% of [Israeli Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Christians) belonged to the Melkite Greek Catholic Church,[^cite_note-MFA2014-161] while around 30% of Israeli Christians belonged to the [Greek Orthodox Church of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Patriarchate_of_Jerusalem).[^cite_note-MFA2014-161] The Christian communities in Israel run numerous [schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_school), [colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colleges), [hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitals), [clinics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinics), [orphanages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orphanages), homes for the elderly, [dormitories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormitories), family and youth centers, [hotels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotels), and guesthouses.[^cite_note-McGahern_2011_51-162]

[Nazareth](21702.md) has the largest Christian Arab population, followed by [Haifa](55606.md).[^cite_note-Christian2020-159] The majority of Haifa's Arab minority is Christian as well.[^cite_note-NYT01416-163] The Christian Arab communities in Nazareth and Haifa tend to be wealthier and better educated compare to other Arabs elsewhere in Israel.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] Arab Christians also live in a number of other localities in the [Galilee](12639.md); such as [Abu Snan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Snan), [Arraba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arraba,_Israel), [Bi'ina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bi'ina), [Deir Hanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_Hanna), [I'billin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I'billin), [Jadeidi-Makr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jadeidi-Makr), [Kafr Kanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Kanna), [Mazra'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazra'a), [Muqeible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muqeible), [Ras al-Ein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_al-Ein), [Reineh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reineh), [Sakhnin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhnin), [Shefa-Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shefa-Amr), [Tur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tur'an) and [Yafa an-Naseriyye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yafa_an-Naseriyye).[^cite_note-166] localities such as [Eilabun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eilabun), [Jish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jish), [Kafr Yasif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Yasif) and [Rameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rameh) are predominantly Christians.[^cite_note-167] Nearly all the population of [Fassuta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fassuta) and [Mi'ilya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi'ilya) are [Melkite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melkite_Greek_Catholic_Church) [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians).[^cite_note-ChristianYnet-168] Some Druze villages, such as [Daliyat al-Karmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daliyat_al-Karmel),[^cite_note-169] [Ein Qiniyye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_Qiniyye), [Hurfeish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurfeish), [Isfiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfiya), [Kisra-Sumei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kisra-Sumei), [Maghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghar,_Israel), [Majdal Shams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majdal_Shams) and [Peki'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peki'in) have small Christian Arab populations.[^cite_note-Statistics-122] Mixed cities such as [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel), [Jerusalem](16043.md), [Lod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lod), [Ma'alot-Tarshiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'alot-Tarshiha), [Nof HaGalil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nof_HaGalil), [Ramla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramla) and [Tel Aviv](31453.md)-[Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa) have significant Christian Arab populations.[^cite_note-Statistics-122]

Many Christian Arabs have been prominent in Arab political parties in Israel, and leaders have included Archbishop [George Hakim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximos_V_Hakim), [Emile Toma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emile_Toma), [Tawfik Toubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tawfik_Toubi), [Emile Habibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emile_Habibi), and [Azmi Bishara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azmi_Bishara). Notable Christian religious figures include the [Melkite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melkite_Greek_Catholic_Church) Archbishops of the Galilee [Elias Chacour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elias_Chacour) and [Boutros Mouallem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boutros_Mouallem), the [Latin Patriarch of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Patriarch_of_Jerusalem) [Michel Sabbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Sabbah), and Bishop [Munib Younan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munib_Younan) of the Lutheran Church of Jordan and the Holy Land. Israeli Supreme Court judges [Salim Joubran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salim_Joubran) and [George Karra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Karra) are Christian Arab.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] Notable Christian figures in science and high tech include [Hossam Haick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hossam_Haick)[^cite_note-172] who has a number of contributions in multidisciplinary fields such as [nanotechnology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanotechnology), [nanosensors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanosensors) and [molecular electronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_electronics),[^cite_note-173] and [Johny Srouji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johny_Srouji) who is [Apple](856.md)'s [senior vice president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_vice_president) of [hardware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_hardware) [technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technologies).[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

Among Arab Christians in Israel, some emphasize [pan-Arabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism), whilst a small minority enlists in the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md).[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] Since September 2014 Christian families or clans who have either Aramaic/Assyrian or Maronite cultural heritage are considered an ethnicity separate from [Israeli Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Arabs) and can register themselves as Arameans. This recognition comes after about seven years of activity by the Aramean Christian Foundation in Israel, which rather than sticking to an Arab identity, wishes to assimilate into an Israeli lifestyle. Aram is led by IDF Major Shadi Khalloul Risho and the Israeli Christian Recruitment Forum, headed by [Father Gabriel Naddaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Father_Gabriel_Naddaf) of the Greek-Orthodox Church and Major Ihab Shlayan.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] The move was condemned by the Greek Orthodox Patriarchate, which described it as an attempt to divide the Palestinian minority in Israel.[^cite_note-182] Other pro-Zionist advocates supporting similar ideas received extensive coverage in Israeli state sponsored media and Jewish news outlets to severe criticisms from their co-religionists (see [Yoseph Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoseph_Haddad)).

[Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) are one of the most educated groups in Israel.[^cite_note-183] Statistically, [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) in Israel have the highest rates of [educational attainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_attainment) among all religious communities, according to a data by [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) in 2010, 63% of Israeli [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) have had college or [postgraduate education](153981.md), the highest of any religious and [ethno-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-religious) group.[^cite_note-bokra.net-184] Despite the fact that Arab Christians only represent 2% of the total Israeli population, in 2014 they accounted for 17% of the country's [university](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University) students, and for 14% of its [college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College) students.[^cite_note-185] There are more [Christians](18337522.md) who have attained a [bachelor's degree](188874.md) or higher [academic degrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_degree) than the median Israeli population.[^cite_note-Christians_in_Israel:_Strong_in_education-158] The rate of students studying in the field of [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine) was higher among [Christian](18337522.md) Arab students than that of all other sectors,[^cite_note-186] and the percentage of [Arab Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christian) women who are receiving higher education is also higher than that of other groups.[^cite_note-המגזר_הערבי_נוצרי_הכי_מצליח_במערכת_החינוך-187]



[Catholic school](1452366.md) in [Haifa](55606.md): High level [Christian schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_school) are among Israel's best performing educational institutions.

[^cite_note-Why_Angry_Christians_in_Israel_Are-188]

The [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) noted that when taking into account the data recorded over the years, Israeli [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) fared the best in terms of education in comparison to any other group receiving an education in Israel.[^cite_note-Christians_in_Israel:_Strong_in_education-158][^cite_note-189] In 2012 Christian Arabs had the highest rates of success at [matriculation examinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matriculation_examination),[^cite_note-TOI2013-190] In 2016, Arab Christians had the highest rates of success at [matriculation examinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matriculation_examination), namely 73.9%, both in comparison to Muslim and [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_in_Israel) Israelis (41% and 51.9% respectively), and to the students from the different branches of the [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew) (majority [Jewish](25955086.md)) education system considered as one group (55.1%).[^cite_note-Aruz7-191]

In terms of their socio-economic situation, [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) are more similar to the Jewish population than to the Muslim Arab population.[^cite_note-192] They have the lowest incidence of poverty and the lowest percentage of unemployment, at 4.9%, compared to 6.5% among Jewish men and women.[^cite_note-193] They have also the highest [median household income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_household_income) among Arab citizens of Israel and second highest median household income among the Israeli ethno-religious groups.[^cite_note-194] Also Arab Christians have a high presentation in [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science) and in the [white collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) professions.[^cite_note-195] In Israel Arab Christians are portrayed as a hard working and [upper middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_middle_class) educated [ethno-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-religious) minority. According to study the majority of Christians in Israel (68.2 per cent) are employed in the service sector, i.e. banks, insurance companies, schools, tourism, hospitals etc.[^cite_note-McGahern_2011_51-162]

According to the study "Are Christian Arabs the New Israeli Jews? Reflections on the Educational Level of Arab Christians in Israel" by Hanna David from the [University of Tel Aviv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tel_Aviv), one of the factors why [Israeli Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel) are the most educated segment of Israel's population is the high level of the Christian educational institutions. [Christian schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_school) in Israel are among the best schools in the country, and while those schools represent only 4% of the Arab schooling sector, about 34% of Arab university students come from [Christian schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_school),[^cite_note-SJ-196] and about 87% of the Israeli Arabs in the [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech) sector have been educated in Christian schools.[^cite_note-middleeasteye.net-197][^cite_note-Why_Angry_Christians_in_Israel_Are-188] A 2011 Maariv article described the Christian Arab sector as "the most successful in the education system",[^cite_note-המגזר_הערבי_נוצרי_הכי_מצליח_במערכת_החינוך-187] an opinion supported by the [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) and others who point out that Christian Arabs fared best in terms of education in comparison to any other group receiving an education in Israel.[^cite_note-Christians_in_Israel:_Strong_in_education-158]

### Lebanese people

Main article: [Lebanese people in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people_in_Israel)

See also: [Maronites in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maronites_in_Israel)

There are 3,500 [Lebanese people in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people_in_Israel),[^cite_note-Haaretz_2021-198] most of them are former members of the [South Lebanon Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lebanon_Army) (SLA) and their families. The SLA was a Christian-dominated militia allied with the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md) during the [South Lebanon conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lebanon_conflict_\(1985%E2%80%932000\)) until Israel's withdrawal from Lebanon in May 2000 that ended the [Israeli occupation of Southern Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_occupation_of_Southern_Lebanon_\(1982%E2%80%932000\)).[^cite_note-Haaretz_2021-198] The majority are [Maronites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maronites) but there are also Muslims, Druze and Christians of other denominations among them.[^cite_note-Shachmon_2019-199] They are registered by the [Ministry of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(Israel\)) as "Lebanese" and hold [Israeli citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_citizenship_law).[^cite_note-Haaretz_2021-198] They are located across the country, mainly in the [Northern District](324281.md), in cities such as [Nahariya](323332.md), [Kiryat Shmona](323369.md), [Tiberias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberias), and [Haifa](55606.md).[^cite_note-Shachmon_2019-199]

The native language of former SLA members is [Lebanese Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Arabic). However, the language is only partially transmitted from one generation to another. The majority of the second generation understand and speak Lebanese Arabic but are unable to read and write it. Young Lebanese Israeli mainly [text](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Text_messaging) in Hebrew or, more rarely, in Lebanese Arabic written in the [Hebrew alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_alphabet). Religious books for children and youths are similarly written in [Classical Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic) (or in Lebanese Arabic for some songs) in Hebrew letters.[^cite_note-Shachmon_2019-199]

## Population

See also: [Demographics of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Israel)



Arabs in Israel population pyramid in 2021



The lower town of [Haifa](55606.md), an area where Arabs, both Christians and Muslims, comprise around 70% of the residents.

[^cite_note-cbs.govs-200]

In 2006, the official number of Arab residents in Israel was 1,413,500 people, about 20% of Israel's population. This figure includes 209,000 Arabs (14% of the Israeli Arab population) in East Jerusalem, also counted in the Palestinian statistics, although 98% of East Jerusalem Palestinians have either Israeli residency or Israeli citizenship.[^cite_note-http-201] In 2012, the official number of Arab residents in Israel increased to 1,617,000 people, about 21% of Israel's population.[^cite_note-202] The Arab population in 2023 was estimated at 2,065,000 people, representing 21% of the country's population.[^cite_note-CBS2023-1]

According to the Israeli [Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bureau_of_Statistics_\(Israel\)) census in 2010, "the Arab population lives in 134 towns and villages. About 44 percent of them live in towns (compared to 81 percent of the Jewish population); 48 percent live in villages with local councils (compared to 9 percent of the Jewish population). Four percent of the Arab citizens live in small villages with regional councils, while the rest live in unrecognized villages (the proportion is much higher, 31 percent in the Negev)".[^cite_note-Research-203] The Arab population in Israel is located in five main areas: [Galilee](12639.md) (54.6% of total Israeli Arabs), [Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_\(Israel\)) (23.5% of total Israeli Arabs), [Golan Heights](7515865.md), [East Jerusalem](343508.md), and Northern [Negev](142273.md) (13.5% of total Israeli Arabs).[^cite_note-Research-203] Around 8.4% (approximately 102,000 inhabitants) of Israeli Arabs live in officially mixed Jewish-Arab cities (excluding Arab residents in East Jerusalem), including [Haifa](55606.md), [Lod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lod), [Ramle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramle), [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa)-[Tel Aviv](31453.md), [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel), [Nof HaGalil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nof_HaGalil), and [Ma'alot Tarshiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'alot_Tarshiha).[^cite_note-204]



[Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa), which currently has 16,000 Arab residents, mixed of Muslims and Christians.

[^cite_note-205]



Old City of [Acre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acre,_Israel), an area where Arabs make up 95% of the residents.

[^cite_note-cbs.govs-200]

In Israel's [Northern District](324281.md)[^cite_note-CBS2019-6] Arab citizens of Israel form a majority of the population (52%) and about 50% of the Arab population lives in 114 different localities throughout Israel.[^cite_note-arabhra.org-206] In total there are 122 primarily if not entirely [Arab localities in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_localities_in_Israel), 89 of them having populations over two thousand.[^cite_note-Statistics-122] The seven townships as well as the [Abu Basma Regional Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Basma_Regional_Council) that have been constructed by the government for the Bedouin population of the Negev,[^cite_note-207] are the only Arab localities to have been established since 1948,[^cite_note-Stern-208] with the aim of relocating the Arab Bedouin citizens (*see preceding section on [Bedouin](#Muslim)*).[^cite_note-Bedouin-130]

46% of the country's Arabs (622,400 people) live in predominantly Arab communities in the north.[^cite_note-CBS2019-6] In 2024 [Nazareth](21702.md) was the largest Arab city, with a population of 75,704,[^cite_note-Israelpopulations-209] roughly 40,000 of whom are Muslim. [Shefa-'Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shefa-'Amr) has a population of approximately 43,858 and the city is mixed with sizable populations of Muslims, Christians, and Druze.

[Jerusalem](16043.md), a mixed city, has the largest overall Arab population. Jerusalem housed 332,600 Arabs in 2016 (37.7% of the city's residents)[^cite_note-210] and together with the local council of [Abu Ghosh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Ghosh), some 19% of the country's entire Arab population.

14% of Arab citizens live in the [Haifa District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_District) predominantly in the [Wadi Ara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Ara) region. Here is the largest Muslim city, [Umm al-Fahm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_al-Fahm), with a population of 60,209. [Baqa-Jatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baqa-Jatt) is the second largest Arab population center in the district. The city of [Haifa](55606.md) has an Arab population of 10%, much of it in the [Wadi Nisnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Nisnas), Abbas and Halissa neighborhoods.[^cite_note-AR-211] [Wadi Nisnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Nisnas) and Abbas neighborhoods, are largely [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel),[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-Daniel_Lefkowitz-213] Halisa and [Kababir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kababir) are largely [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Israel).[^cite_note-Daniel_Lefkowitz-213]

10% of the country's Arab population resides in the [Central District](844282.md) of Israel, primarily the cities of [Tayibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tayibe), [Tira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tira,_Israel), and [Qalansawe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qalansawe) as well as the mixed cities of [Lod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lod) and [Ramla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramla) which have mainly Jewish populations.[^cite_note-Statistics-122]

Of the remaining 11%, 10% live in Bedouin communities in the northwestern [Negev](142273.md). The Bedouin city of [Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahat) is the only Arab city in the [Southern District](844338.md) and it is the third largest Arab city in Israel.
