Statistically, [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) in Israel have the highest rates of [educational attainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_attainment) among all religious communities, according to a data by [Israel Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) in 2010, 63% of Israeli [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) have had college or [postgraduate education](153981.md), the highest of any religious and [ethno-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethno-religious) group.[^cite_note-bokra.net-184] According to the Israeli [Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bureau_of_Statistics_\(Israel\)) census in 2020, 70.9% of [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Israel) in Israel have a [college degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_degree),[^cite_note-395] followed by [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Druze) (15.3%),[^cite_note-DP-139] and [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Israel) (10%).[^cite_note-Muslim2020-125]

Among Israeli higher education institutions the [University of Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Haifa) has the largest percentage (41%) of Arab-Israeli students.[^cite_note-396] The [Technion Israel Institute of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technion_Israel_Institute_of_Technology) has the second largest percentage (22.2%) of Arab-Israeli students.[^cite_note-Harman-391][^cite_note-Outstanding_Arab_Students-392]

### Teaching staff in Hebrew schools

As of 2026, data from the Central Bureau of Statistics indicated that the number of Arab teachers employed in the Hebrew-language education system had increased steadily over the past decade, reaching nearly 4,000 and accounting for approximately 4% of the workforce. By the early 2020s, close to half of Hebrew-language public schools employed at least one Arab teacher, and the proportion of Arab teachers among new hires continued to rise.[^cite_note-397]

## Military conscription

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(November 2024)* |
| --- | --- |



Bedouin IDF soldiers of Rumat al-Heib (عرب الهيب) during a military parade in Tel-Aviv in June 1949

Unlike their Jewish counterparts, Arab citizens are not [required to serve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Israel) in the Israeli military, and, outside the Bedouin community, few (around 120 a year) volunteer.[^cite_note-Statistics-122] Until 2000, each year between 5–10% of the [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Bedouin) population of draft age volunteered for the [Israel Defense Forces](39237.md) (IDF), and Bedouin were well known for their unique status as volunteers. The legendary Israeli soldier, [Amos Yarkoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amos_Yarkoni), first commander of the Shaked Reconnaissance Battalion in the [Givati Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Givati_Brigade), was a Bedouin (born *Abd el-Majid Hidr*). Bedouin soldiers dominate the elite human tracking units that guard Israel's northern and southern border.[^cite_note-bedouin_in_israel-398] Lieutenant Colonel Magdi Mazarib, a Bedouin, who is the Israeli army's highest-ranking tracking commander, told the [AFP](471981.md) that he believes that "the state of Bedouin in Israel is better, as far as the respect we get, our progress, education".[^cite_note-bedouin_in_israel-398] In 2004 the number of Bedouin in the army may have been be less than 1%.[^cite_note-399] A 2003 report stated that willingness among Bedouin to serve in the army had drastically dropped in recent years, as the Israeli government has failed to fulfill promises of equal service provision to Bedouin citizens.[^cite_note-caliber1-400] However, a 2009 article in [Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz) stated that volunteer recruitment for a crack elite Bedouin army unit rose threefold.[^cite_note-401]

IDF figures indicate that, in 2002 and 2003, Christians represented 0.1 percent of all recruits. In 2004, the number of recruits had doubled. Altogether, in 2003, the percentage of Christians serving had grown by 16 percent over the year 2000. The IDF does not publish figures on the exact number of recruits by religious denomination, and in 2005 it was estimated that a few dozen Christians currently served in the IDF.[^cite_note-Christian_Arabs-144]



[Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) commander of the IDF Herev battalion

The Druze are required to serve in the IDF in accordance with an agreement between their local religious leaders and the Israeli government in 1956. Opposition to the decision among the Druze populace was evident immediately, but was unsuccessful in reversing the decision.[^cite_note-402] Multiple Druze in the IDF become officers[^cite_note-403] and some rising to general officer rank.[^cite_note-404] In recent years, a growing minority from within the Druze community have denounced this mandatory enrollment, and refused to serve.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] In 2001, [Said Nafa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Said_Nafa), who identifies as a Palestinian Druze and serves as the head of the Balad party's national council, founded the "Pact of Free Druze", an organization that aims "to stop the conscription of the Druze and claims the community is an inalienable part of the Arabs in Israel and the Palestinian nation at large".[^cite_note-407]

### National service

Rather than perform army service, Israeli Arab youths have the option to volunteer to [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherut_Leumi) and receive benefits similar to those received by discharged soldiers. The volunteers are generally allocated to Arab populations, where they assist with social and community matters. As of 2010 there are 1,473 Arabs volunteering for national service. According to sources in the national service administration, Arab leaders are counseling youths to refrain from performing services to the state. According to a National Service official: "For years the Arab leadership has demanded, justifiably, benefits for Arab youths similar to those received by discharged soldiers. Now, when this opportunity is available, it is precisely these leaders who reject the state's call to come and do the service, and receive these benefits."[^cite_note-ynetnews2-300]

## Intercommunal relations

### Surveys and polls

In a 2004 survey by Sammy Smooha of the [University of Haifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Haifa) [Jewish-Arab Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish-Arab_Center), 85% of Israeli Arabs stated that Israel has a right to exist as an independent state, and 70% that it has a right to exist as a democratic, Jewish state.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409] A [Truman Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Institute) survey from 2005 found that 63% of the Arab citizens accepted the principle that Israel is the state of the Jewish people.[^cite_note-Shamir-374][^cite_note-410]

A 2006 poll by the Arab advocacy group *the Center Against Racism* showed negative attitudes towards Arabs. The poll found that 63% of Jews believe Arabs are a security threat; 68% would refuse to live in the same building as an Arab; 34% believe that Arab culture is inferior to Israeli culture. Support for segregation between Jewish and Arab citizens was higher among Jews of Middle Eastern origin.[^cite_note-411]

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Very patriotic |  | 17% |
| Patriotic |  | 7% |
| Somewhat patriotic |  | 35% |
| Not especially patriotic |  | 41% |
| Herzliya patriotism survey[^cite_note-pat-412] |  |  |

In a 2006 patriotism survey, 56% of Israeli Arabs were not proud of their citizenship and 73% were not ready to fight to defend the state, but 77% said that Israel was better than most other countries and 53% were proud of the country's welfare system. Eighty-two percent said they would rather be a citizen of Israel than of any other country in the world.[^cite_note-pat-412]

An [Israeli Democracy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Democracy_Institute) (IDI) poll in 2007 showed that 75% of "Israeli Arabs would support a constitution that maintained Israel's status as a Jewish and democratic state while guaranteeing equal rights for minorities, while 23% said they would oppose such a definition".[^cite_note-poll-413] Another survey that year showed that 62% of Israel's Arabs would prefer to remain Israeli citizens rather than become citizens of a future Palestinian state.[^cite_note-IPOU-414] A separate 2008 poll found that 77% would rather live in Israel than in any other country in the world.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416] Another 2007 poll by Sammy Smooha found that 63% of Jewish Israelis avoided entering Arab towns and cities; 68% feared the possibility of widespread civil unrest among Israeli Arabs; 50% of Israeli Arabs justified Hezbollah's capture of IDF reservists [Ehud Goldwasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehud_Goldwasser) and [Eldad Regev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eldad_Regev) in a cross-border raid; 19% thought Israel was justified in going to war following the kidnapping; 48% justified Hezbollah rocket attacks on northern Israel during the [2006 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War); 89% of Israeli Arabs saw the IDF bombing of Lebanon as a war crime, while 44% of Israeli Arabs viewed Hezbollah's bombing of Israel as a war crime; 62% of Israeli Arabs worried that Israel could transfer their communities to the jurisdiction of a future Palestinian state, and 60% said they were concerned about a possible mass expulsion; 76% of Israeli Arabs described [Zionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zionism) as racist; 68% of Israeli Arabs would be content to live in the Jewish state alongside a Palestinian state in the West Bank and Gaza Strip; 41% of Israeli Arab citizens [denied the Holocaust ever happened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial).[^cite_note-Poll_a-417]

In 2007, the *[Association for Civil Rights in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Civil_Rights_in_Israel)* reported a "dramatic increase" in racism against Arab citizens, including a 26 percent rise in anti-Arab incidents. ACRI president [Sami Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sami_Michael) said that "Israeli society is reaching new heights of racism that damages freedom of expression and privacy".[^cite_note-ReferenceA-418]

A 2008 poll on intercommunal relations by Harvard [Kennedy School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_School_of_Government) found that Arabs and Jews in Israel underestimated the extent to which their communities "liked" one another. 68% of the Jews supported teaching Arabic in Jewish schools.[^cite_note-419]

A 2008 poll by the Center Against Racism found that 75% of Israeli Jews would not live in a building with Arabs; over 60% would not invite Arabs to their homes; 40% believed that Arabs should be stripped of the [right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffrage); over 50% agreed that the state should encourage emigration of Arab citizens to other countries; 59% considered Arab culture primitive. Asked "What do you feel when you hear people speaking Arabic?" 31% said hate and 50% said fear. Only 19% reported positive or neutral feelings.[^cite_note-420]

Surveys in 2009 found a radicalization in the positions of Israeli Arabs towards the State of Israel, with 41% of Israeli Arabs recognizing Israel's right to exist as a [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeland_for_the_Jewish_people) and democratic state (down from 66% in 2003), and 54% believing Israel has a right to exist as an independent country (down from 81% in 2003). Polls also showed that 40% of Arab citizens engaged in [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial).[^cite_note-Poll_a-417]

A 2010 poll of Israeli high school students found that 50% did not think Israeli Arabs were entitled to the same rights as Jews in Israel, and 56% thought Arabs should not be elected to the [Knesset](74656.md).[^cite_note-Poll2010-421] The figures rose among religious students.[^cite_note-422]

A 2010 Arab Jewish Relations Survey, compiled by Prof. Sami Smoocha in collaboration with the Jewish-Arab Center at the University of Haifa shows that 71% Arab citizens of Israel said they blamed Jews for the hardships suffered by Palestinians during and after the "Nakba" in 1948. 38% denied the Holocaust. The percentage supporting the use of violence to advance Arab causes climbed from 6% in 1995 to 12% in 2010. 66% say they reject Israel as a Jewish and Zionist state, while 30% opposed its existence under any terms. 63% saw the Jews as "foreign settlers who do not fit into the region and will eventually leave, when the land will return to the Palestinians".[^cite_note-423]

A 2010 University of Maryland / Zogby International poll of 600 Arab Israelis compiled by [Shibley Telhami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shibley_Telhami) found that 36 percent considered their Arab identity to be "most important", while 22% answered "Palestinian", 19% Muslim, and 12% Israeli.[^cite_note-424]

Amongst other things, a 2012 survey by Mada al-Carmel, the Arab Center for Applied Social Research, asked Arab students what obstacles they felt they faced in getting into university: 71% said the psychometric exam was the primary obstacle, while 40% cited "Jewish racism".[^cite_note-2012survey-425] The survey also found that 45 percent of those questioned felt no pride in Israeli achievements in whatever field, with another 13% reporting negative feelings about them.[^cite_note-2012survey-425]

A Pew Research poll released in March 2016 showed that close to half of all Israeli Jews are in favor of "transferring or expelling" Israel's Arab population. 48% of Israeli Jews strongly agree or agree with the idea, and 46% strongly disagree or disagree. The in-person polling was conducted in a relatively "calm" from late 2014 through early 2015.[^cite_note-426]

In December 2022, support for a two-state solution was 33% among Palestinians, 34% among Israeli Jews, and 60% among Israeli Arabs.[^cite_note-427] At the end of October 2023, after the [Hamas-led attack on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel), the two-state solution had the support of 71.9% of Israeli Arabs and 28.6% of Israeli Jews.[^cite_note-428] According to a poll published by the [Israel Democracy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Democracy_Institute), only 24% of Israeli Arabs believe that the [IDF](39237.md) is trying to uphold international law in the war between Israel and Hamas.[^cite_note-429]

In December 2023, during the Israel-Hamas war, the Druze were the Israeli Arab minority which most identified with the state of Israel (80%), followed by Christians (73%) and Muslims (62%). For residents of the Negev, most of whom are Bedouin, the figure was 73%.[^cite_note-430]

### Violence against Arab citizens in Israel

Alexander Yakobson of Jerusalem's Hebrew University has said "There is very little actual violence between Israeli Jews and Israeli Arabs. Given the length and the intensity of the conflict, that is both surprising and encouraging."[^cite_note-431]

In the 1956 [Kafr Qasim massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Qasim_massacre), 48 unarmed Arab citizens, returning to their village, were gunned down by an Israel Border Police platoon; a curfew had been imposed, but the villagers were not informed of it. Arab citizens have also been killed by Israeli security forces in the wake of violent demonstrations and riots, such as the March 1976 [Land Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_Day) demonstrations, which left 6 dead, and the [October 2000 events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2000_events) in which 12 Israeli Arabs and one Palestinian from Gaza were killed.

In 2005 an [AWOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AWOL) IDF soldier, [Eden Natan-Zada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eden_Natan-Zada) opened fire in a bus in [Shefa-Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shefa-Amr) in northern Israel, murdering four Arabs and wounding twenty-two others. No group had taken credit for the terrorist attack and an official in the settler movement denounced it.[^cite_note-432]

Arab activists have complained about the police abandoning Arab towns to intra communal violence at the hands of common criminals and murderers, calling for cooperation with Israeli police and internal security services under the slogan of "Arab Lives Matter".[^cite_note-433]

### Arab victims of terrorism



Israeli President [Isaac Herzog](4645588.md) in the city of [Rahat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahat) with heads of the [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Bedouin) community and families of kidnapped and murdered Bedouins during the [Hamas-led attack on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Hamas-led_attack_on_Israel) on 7 October 2023

Arab citizens have also been victims of Palestinian, Arab, or Islamist attacks on Israel and Israelis. For example, on 12 September 1956, three [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) guards were killed in an attack on Ein Ofarim, in the [Arabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabah) region.[^cite_note-434] Two Arab citizens were killed in the [Ma'alot massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'alot_massacre) carried out by the [Democratic Front for the Liberation of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Front_for_the_Liberation_of_Palestine) on 15 May 1974. In March 2002, a resident of the Arab town of [Tur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tur'an) was killed in an attack on a Haifa restaurant.[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] Two months later, a woman from [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa) was killed in a [Hamas](13913.md) [suicide bombing](18835454.md) in [Rishon LeZion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishon_LeZion).[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] On 18 June 2002, a woman from the Arab border town of [Barta'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barta'a) was one of 19 killed by Hamas in the [Pat Junction Bus Bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Junction_Bus_Bombing) in [Jerusalem](16043.md).[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] In August 2002, a man from the Arab town of [Mghar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghar,_Israel) and woman from the Druze village of [Sajur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajur) were killed in a suicide bombing at [Meron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meron,_Israel) junction.[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] On 21 October 2002, an [Isfiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmel_City) man and a [Tayibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tayibe) woman were among 14 killed by [Islamic Jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Jihad_Movement_in_Palestine) in the [Egged bus 841 massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karkur_junction_suicide_bombing).[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] On 5 March 2003, a 13-year-old girl from the Druze town of [Daliyat al-Karmel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daliyat_al-Karmel) was one of 17 killed in the [Haifa bus 37 suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haifa_bus_37_suicide_bombing).[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] In May 2003, a [Jisr az-Zarqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jisr_az-Zarqa) man was killed in an [Afula](323344.md) mall suicide bombing.[^cite_note-Haaretz-435] On 19 March 2004, [Fatah](11104.md) [al-Aqsa Martyrs' Brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa_Martyrs'_Brigades) gunmen killed [George Khoury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Khoury_\(murder_victim\)), a [Hebrew University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem) student.[^cite_note-436] On 12 December 2004, five Arab IDF soldiers were killed in an explosion and shooting at the border with Egypt for which the [Fatah Hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatah_Hawks) claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-437] On 4 October 2003, four Arab citizens of Israel were among the 21 killed by [Hanadi Jaradat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanadi_Jaradat) in the [Maxim restaurant suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_restaurant_suicide_bombing). In July 2006, 19 Arab citizens were killed due to [Hezbollah](13919.md) rocket fire in the course of the [2006 Lebanon War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Lebanon_War).

On 22 August 2006, 11 Arab tourists from Israel were killed when their [bus overturned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_bus_crash) in Egypt's [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula). Israel sent [Magen David Adom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magen_David_Adom), but the ambulances waited for hours at the border before receiving Egyptian permission to enter and treat the wounded, responsible for at least one of the deaths. The victims say that the driver acted as part of a planned terrorist attack, and are attempting to receive compensation from the government.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

## Relations with other Palestinians

During the [2021 Israel–Palestine crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_crisis), according to [Al Arabiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Arabiya_English), Fatah backed a call for a general strike on 18 May 2021 in the West Bank, including East Jerusalem and Palestinians in Israel were asked to take part.[^cite_note-440] In an unusual display of unity by "Palestinian citizens of Israel, who make up 20% of its population, and those in the territories Israel seized in 1967"[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443] the strike went ahead and "shops were shuttered across cities in Gaza, the occupied West Bank and in villages and towns inside Israel".[^cite_note-444]

## Culture



[Al-Midan Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Midan_Theater) in [Haifa](55606.md)

Many Arab citizens of Israel share in the culture of the [Palestinian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people) and wider Arab region of which a number of them form a part. There are still some women who produce Palestinian cultural products such as [Palestinian embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatreez),[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] and [costume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_costumes). The Palestinian folk dance, known as the [dabke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dabke), continues to be taught to youth in cultural groups, and is often danced at weddings and other parties.

### Cultural centers



The house of culture and art in [Nazareth](21702.md)

As the largest Arab city in Israel, [Nazareth](21702.md) is a cultural, political, economic and commercial center of the Arab citizens of Israel, and became also a center of Arab and [Palestinian nationalism](47246228.md).[^cite_note-447] Haifa, however, is the center of liberal [Arabic-speaking culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture), as it was under British colonial rule. Arabs in Haifa (mostly Christians and Muslims) tend to be wealthier and better educated compared to Arabs elsewhere in Israel.[^cite_note-NYT01416-163] An active Arab cultural life has developed in the 21st century.[^cite_note-NYT01416-163] Haifa is center of a number of Arab-owned businesses such as [theaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theaters), [bars](272207.md), cafes, [restaurants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restaurants) and [nightclubs](19321330.md) which host also a different cultural discussions and art exhibitions.[^cite_note-NYT01416-163]

Other centers of Arab and Palestinian cultures includes [Kafr Yasif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Yasif) and [Rameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rameh). According to the historian [Atallah Mansour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atallah_Mansour?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Kafr Yasif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Yasif) is the "most academic Arab town in Israel",[^cite_note-448] while journalist Sylvia Smith calls it "the preeminent \[Arab] cultural town".[^cite_note-SmithBBC-449] With the near total depopulation of the Palestinian Arabs in the major cities of Haifa and Jaffa as a result of the 1948 war, Kafr Yasif and [Rameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rameh) became one of a few villages in the newly established state of Israel to emerge as a central space for Arab culture and politics.[^cite_note-Shihade456-450] Its schools, proximity and location between major cities and other Arab villages, the relatively equal distribution of land ownership among its households and the diversity brought about by the influx of internally displaced Palestinians all contributed to its local importance.[^cite_note-Shihade456-450]

In 1948, it was the only Arab locality in the Galilee to contain a high school outside of the cities of Nazareth, Shefa-Amr and Haifa.[^cite_note-Shihade456-450] Following the war, the high school enrolled students from over fifty Arab villages.[^cite_note-Shihade456-450] Several students, including [Mahmoud Darwish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud_Darwish), became well-known poets, and the village hosted weekly poetry recitals.[^cite_note-Shihade456-450] As of the 1960s, the people of [Rameh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rameh) have been noted for their high levels of education and standards of living.

### Language

Further information: [Arabic language in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language_in_Israel)



A wedding groom and his horse, Jisr az-Zarka, 2009

Linguistically speaking, the majority of Arabic citizens of Israel are fluently bilingual, speaking both a [Palestinian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Arabic) dialect and [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language). In Arab homes and towns, the primary language spoken is Arabic. Some Hebrew words have entered the colloquial Arabic dialect. For example, Arabs often use the word *beseder* (equivalent of "Okay") while speaking Arabic. Other Hebrew words that are regularly interspersed are *ramzor* (stoplight), *mazgan* (air conditioner), and *mahshev* (computer). The resulting dialect is usually referred to as 'Israeli Arabic'.

Such borrowings are often "Arabized" to reflect not only [Arabic phonology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_phonology) but the phonology of Hebrew as spoken by Arabs. For example, the second consonant of מעונות (*me'onot*, "dormitory") would be pronounced as a [voiced pharyngeal fricative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiced_pharyngeal_fricative) rather than the [glottal stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glottal_stop) traditionally used by the vast majority of Israeli Jews. A survey by the Central Bureau of Statistics released in 2013 found that 60% of [Israeli Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Arabs) were proficient or highly proficient in Hebrew, while 17% could not read it and 12% could not speak it.[^cite_note-ynet-451]

There are different local colloquial dialects among Arabs in different regions and localities. For example, the [Little Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triangle_\(Israel\)) residents of [Umm al-Fahm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_al-Fahm) are known for pronouncing the [kaph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaph) sound with a "ch" (as-in-cheese) rather than "k" (as-in-kite). Some Arabic words or phrases are used only in their respective localities, such as the Nazareth word for "now" which is *issa*, and *silema* a local modification of the English word "cinema".[^cite_note-452][^cite_note-453] The [Druze Arabic dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_Arabic_dialect?action=edit\&redlink=1), especially in the villages, is often different from the other regional Israeli Arabic dialects. Druze Arabic dialect is distinguished from others by retention of the phoneme /*[qāf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qoph)*/.[^cite_note-454] They often use [Hebrew characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_script) to write their Arabic dialect online.[^cite_note-455]

Arab citizens of Israel tend to watch both the Arab satellite news stations and Israeli cable stations and read both Arabic and Hebrew newspapers, comparing the information against one another.[^cite_note-456]

### Music and art

See also: [Palestinian artists in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_art#Palestinian_artists_in_Israel)



[DAM band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAM_\(band\))

The [Palestinian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_art) scene in general has been supported by the contributions of Arab citizens of Israel.[^cite_note-Zvi-457] In addition to the contribution of artists such as singer [Rim Banna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rim_Banna) (from [Nazareth](21702.md)) and [Amal Murkus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amal_Murkus) (from [Kafr Yasif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Yasif)) to evolving traditional [Palestinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_music) and [Arabic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music) styles, a new generation of Arab youth in Israel has also begun asserting a Palestinian identity in new musical forms. For instance of the Palestinian hip hop group [DAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAM_\(band\)), from Lod, has spurred the emergence of other hip hop groups from Akka, to Bethlehem, to Ramallah, to Gaza City. [DAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DAM_\(band\)) is the first [Palestinian hip hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_hip_hop) group.[^cite_note-BBC-458]

Singer-songwriter such as [Nasrin Kadri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasrin_Kadri), [Mira Awad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mira_Awad), [Lina Makhul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lina_Makhul), Luay Ali, Sharif "the druze boy" have put Arab citizens of Israel on the musical map.[^cite_note-459]

In the art scene, the Palestinian minority in Israel has asserted its identity according to Ben Zvi, who suggests that this group of artists who are identified "on the one hand, as part of a broad Palestinian cultural system, and on the other — in a differentiated manner — as the Palestinian minority in Israel."[^cite_note-Zvi-457] The issue of identity becomes particularly clear in an artwork of the Palestinian artist [Raafat Hattab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raafat_Hattab?action=edit\&redlink=1) from [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa). The video performance "untitled" was part of the exhibition "Men in the Sun" in the Herzliya Museum of Contemporary Art in 2009. In the work, Raafat Hattab is seen as he pours water into a bucket in order to lengthily water an olive tree which is a sign for the lost paradise before [1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Palestinian_exodus). The scene is primed by the song *Hob* (Love) by the Lebanese [Ahmad Kaabour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Kaabour) which expresses the need for Palestinian solidarity. The chorus repeats the phrase "I left a place" and it seems as if the video is dealing with memory. But as the camera zooms out, the spectator realizes that Hattab and the olive tree both actually stand in the middle of the [Rabin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabin_Square), a main place in [Tel Aviv](31453.md), and the water used for watering the tree comes from the nearby fountain. "In my installations I appear in different identities that combined are my identity — a Palestinian minority in Israel and a queer minority in the Palestinian culture", explains Rafaat Hattab in an interview with the Tel Avivian City Mouse Magazine.[^cite_note-City_Mouse_Magazine-460] Asim Abu Shaqra's focus of the sabra plant (prickly pear cactus) in his paintings is another example of the centrality of identity, especially vis-a-vie the Palestinian subject's Israeli counterpart, in Palestinian art. Tal Ben Zvi writes that Abu Shaqra is one of the few Palestinian artists, who have succeeded in entering the canon of Israeli art.[^cite_note-LR-461]<sup>: 115 </sup> Abu Shaqra painted various paintings featuring the sabra, both a symbol for the Palestinian Nakba and a symbol for the new Israeli and his work stirred up a debate in the Israeli art discourse over the image of the sabra in Israeli culture and over questions of cultural appropriation and ownership of this image.[^cite_note-LR-461]<sup>: 118 </sup>

### Cuisine



"Doniana" is a popular Arab [seafood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seafood) restaurant in Acre

[Arab cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cuisine) in Israel is similar to other [Levantine cuisines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_cuisine) and is rich in grains, meat, potato, cheese, bread, [whole grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_grain#Varieties), [fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruit#Food_uses), [vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetable#Cultivation), fresh [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_\(food\)) and tomatoes. Perhaps the most distinctive aspect of Israeli Arab and Levantine cuisine is *[meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze)* including *[tabbouleh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabbouleh)*, *[hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus)* and *[baba ghanoush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_ghanoush)*, *[kibbeh nayyeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kibbeh_nayyeh)* is also a popular mezze among Israeli Arabs. Other famous foods among Israeli Arab include [falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel), [sfiha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sfiha), [shawarma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawarma), [dolma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolma), [kibbeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kibbeh), [kusa mahshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stuffed_squash), [shishbarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshpara), [muhammara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammara), and [mujaddara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujaddara).[^cite_note-462] Druze pita is a Druze-styled [pita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pita) filled with [labneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labneh) (thick yoghurt) and topped with [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) and [za'atar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Za'atar),[^cite_note-463] and a popular bread in Israel.[^cite_note-464]

Arabic restaurants are popular in Israel and relatively inexpensive establishments often offer a selection of *[meze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze)* salads followed by grilled meat with a side of "chips" ([french fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries), from [British English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English)) and a simple dessert such as chocolate [mousse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousse) for dessert.[^cite_note-465] "The Old Man and the Sea" is a popular Arab seafood restaurant in the southern part of [Ajami, Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajami,_Jaffa).[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467] [Abu Hassan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hassan_\(restaurant\)) is a small [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus) restaurant located at the northern tip of Ajami. It was opened in 1959 by Ali Karawan and now has two additional branches in Jaffa.[^cite_note-468] It has been rated as the best [hummus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hummus) restaurant in Israel in a number of lists. The restaurant is famous for its loyal clientele of Arabs and Jews.

[Falafel HaZkenim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel_HaZkenim) (Hebrew: "the elders") is an Arab Israeli restaurant and [falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel) stand, located in [Wadi Nisnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Nisnas), [Haifa](55606.md). Since its foundation, guests are greeted with a free falafel ball dipped in [tahini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahini). Foodies consider HaZkenim's [falafel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falafel) among the best in Israel.[^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470][^cite_note-471][^cite_note-472] It was founded in 1950 by George and Najala Afara.[^cite_note-walla-2547349-473] The falafel stand initially had no signage. Jewish customers often said that they would go to "hazkenim" (the old folks) for falafel, while Arab customers identified the place with the female co-owner. When the owners did put up a sign, it contained both names.

### Sport



[Mu'nas Dabbur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'nas_Dabbur), professional [footballer](10568.md) who plays as a [striker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striker_\(association_football\)) for [Bundesliga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundesliga) club [1899 Hoffenheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TSG_1899_Hoffenheim) and the [Israel national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_national_football_team)

Arab citizens of Israel have been prominent in the field of sport, as seen specifically by their contributions to Israeli football.[^cite_note-474] Players such as [Abbas Suan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbas_Suan) have had illustrious careers playing not only professional football, but also most notably for the [Israel national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_national_football_team).[^cite_note-475] These players have been argued to represent symbols of political movement and civil justice for the Arab citizens of Israel, considering alleged discriminatory sentiments against the Arab population.[^cite_note-476] Specifically in a 2005 World Cup Qualifying match, Suan and his Arab teammates played significant roles for the Israeli National Team, where Suan scored an equalising goal against the [Republic of Ireland national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland_national_football_team).[^cite_note-477] Following this match, Arab citizens of Israel received unprecedented media attention from Israeli TV stations. Even without the publicity, the Arab citizens of Israel have been said to look up to these players in hopes that they speak up for them as their political voice within Israel.[^cite_note-Chabin-478] In the following months, Suan and other players were criticised by the Hebrew media for their involvement with political issues.[^cite_note-479] This was due to their answers to questions regarding their partaking in the singing of the [Hatikvah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatikvah), the [national anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_anthem) of Israel.[^cite_note-480]

Overall, Israel-Palestinian soccer players are looked up to greatly by the Arab citizens of Israel, yet are allegedly criticised and silenced when it comes to their attempts to involve themselves in political issues surrounding equality for Arab citizens in Israel.[^cite_note-481]

### Cinema and theater

Arab citizens of Israel have made significant contributions in both Hebrew and Arabic cinema and theater. [Mohammad Bakri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Bakri),[^cite_note-482] [Hiam Abbass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiam_Abbass),[^cite_note-Interview-483] and [Juliano Mer-Khamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliano_Mer-Khamis) have starred in Israeli film and television. Directors such as Mohammad Bakri, [Elia Suleiman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elia_Suleiman), [Hany Abu-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hany_Abu-Assad), [Michel Khleifi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Khleifi), and [Maysaloun Hamoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maysaloun_Hamoud) have put Arab citizens of Israel on the cinematic map.

*Avoda Aravit* (2007), or in English, *[Arab Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Labor)*, is a satirical sitcom written by Kashua and aired on Israel's [Channel 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_2_\(Israel\)). A large part of the dialogue is in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) with Hebrew subtitles. The show holds a mirror up to the racism and ignorance on both sides of the ethnic divide and has been compared with *[All in the Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_in_the_Family)*. The show received overwhelmingly positive reviews, winning awards for Best Comedy, Best Lead Actor in a Comedy, Best Lead Actress in a Comedy, Best Director, and Best Screenplay at the 2013 Israeli Academy of Film and Television awards.[^cite_note-484]

### Literature

Acclaimed Israeli-Arab authors include [Emil Habibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emil_Habibi), [Anton Shammas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Shammas), and [Sayed Kashua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayed_Kashua).

There are a number of self-identification labels currently in use among Palestinian Israelis. Seven of the most commonly used were included in the 2001 survey. They range from "Israeli" and "Israeli Arab", indicating some degree of identification with Israel to "Palestinian," which rejects Israeli identification and wholeheartedly identifies with the Palestinian people. ...\
    &#x20;According to the author's survey, approximately 66 percent of the sample of Palestinian Israelis identified themselves in whole or in part as Palestinian. The modal identity is "Palestinian in Israel", which rejects "Israeli" as a psychological identification, but accepts it as a descriptive label of geographical location. ... \
    &#x20;The establishment-favoured "Israeli Arab" is the second-most popular response in the survey, reflecting its dominance in Israeli social discourse. About 37 percent of respondents identified themselves in some way as "Israeli", double-counting the "Israeli Palestinian" category as both "Israeli" and "Palestinian". Although much smaller than the percentage identifying themselves as Palestinian a nevertheless considerable number include "Israeli" as part of their identity, despite the hardships placed upon them by the Israeli state.

[^cite_note-Amara-39]: &#x20;Muhammad Amara (1999). [*Politics and sociolinguistic reflexes: Palestinian border villages*](https://books.google.com/books?id=LG5seycNTAcC\&q=%22palestinians+of+1948%22\&pg=PA1) (Illustrated ed.). John Benjamins Publishing Company. p. 1. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-90-272-4128-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-90-272-4128-3). Many identity constructs are used to refer to Palestinians in Israel; the Israeli establishment prefer *Israeli Arabs* or *Arabs in Israel*. Others refer to them as *Israeli Palestinians*, *Palestinian Arabs in Israel*, the *Arabs inside the Green Line*. Nowadays the widespread terms among Palestinians are *Palestinians in Israel* or *the Palestinians of 1948*.

[^cite_note-LDE-40]: &#x20;Rebecca B. Kook (2002). [*The Logic of Democratic Exclusion: African Americans in the United States and Palestinian citizens in Israel*](https://books.google.com/books?id=m8USG9mSa-oC\&q=%22palestinians+of+1948%22\&pg=PA68). Lexington Books. pp. 67–68. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-7391-0442-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-7391-0442-2). The category of "Israeli Arab" was constructed by the Israeli authorities. As it indicates, this category assumes and constructs two levels of identity. The first is that of Arab. Local Palestinians who remained in what became Israel were designated as Arabs rather than Palestinians. This category refers to the realm of culture and ethnicity and not, clearly, politics. The official government intention was for the "Arab" to designate culture and ethnicity and the "Israeli" - to designate the political identity. ... In addition to the category of Israeli Arabs, other categories include "the minorities" and "the Arab sector," or, in certain sectors the more cryptic appellation of "our cousins." The use of these labels denies the existence of any type of political or national identification and the use of "minority" even denies them a distinct cultural identity. With the emergence of a more critical discourse ... the categorization expands to include Israeli Palestinians, Palestinians in Israel, Palestinian Arabs, Israeli Palestinian Arabs, the Palestinians of 1948, and so on.

[^cite_note-itfiai-41]: &#x20;["Exploring the Topics of Arab Citizens and Jewish-Arab Relations in Israel"](https://www.iataskforce.org/sites/default/files/resource/resource-2098.pdf) (PDF). *Israeli Arab Task Force*. 2022. Arab citizens' identities are more nuanced than either "Israeli" or "Palestinian." Members of this population group describe themselves (and are described by others) with many terms. Some common terms include: Arab Israelis, Israeli Arabs, Palestinian citizens of Israel, Arab citizens of Israel, 48ers, Palestinian Israelis

[^cite_note-2017poll-42]: &#x20;["Citizenship, Identity and Political Participation: Measuring the Attitudes of the Arab Citizens in Israel"](http://www.kas.de/wf/doc/kas_51217-544-2-30.pdf?171228130254) (PDF). December 2017. pp. 22, 25, 28. The positions of the participants in the focus groups reflect the strength of Palestinian-Arab identity among Arab citizens and the fact that they do not see a contradiction between Palestinian-Arab national identity and Israeli civic identity. The designation "Israeli-Arab" aroused great opposition in the focus groups, as did Israel's Independence Day. A comparison of views expressed in the focus groups with the general results of the survey points to differences between collective positions and memory and individual feelings and attitudes. The collective position presented in the focus group discussions finds expression in the public sphere and emphasizes the Palestinian national identity. Conversely, the responses of the survey participants reveal individual attitudes that assign a broader (albeit secondary, identity) dimension to the component of Israeli civic identity

[^cite_note-43]: &#x20;[Israel's Apartheid Against Palestinians: Cruel System of Domination and Crime Against Humanity](https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2022/02/MDE1551412022ENGLISH.pdf) (PDF) (Report). [Amnesty International](18947898.md). January 2022. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220201215719/https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2022/02/MDE1551412022ENGLISH.pdf) (PDF) from the original on 1 February 2022. Retrieved 20 January 2024. The Israeli Ministry of Foreign Affairs states that "Arab citizens of Israel" is an inclusive term that describes a number of different and primarily Arabic-speaking groups, including Muslim Arabs (this classification includes Bedouins), Christian Arabs, Druze and Circassians. According to the ICBS, at the end of 2019, the Druze population stood at approximately 145,000, while according to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Circassian population totalled 4,000 people. Considering the number of those defined as Muslim Arabs and Christian Arabs together, the population of Palestinian citizens of Israel amounted to around 1.8 million, that is some 20% of the total population in Israel and occupied East Jerusalem.

[^cite_note-44]: &#x20;Smooha, Sammy (2019). ["Still Playing by the Rules"](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1_34-SJamMXAeHAJG6kgZS2WCC0HA1XPN/view). *Index of Arab-Jewish Relations in Israel*. University of Haifa: 87–88. Retrieved 21 December 2023. The three of the nine most attractive identities to the Arabs are: Palestinian Arabs in Israel – 27.1%, Israeli Arabs – 14.9% and Palestinian Arabs – 12.8% These three identities represent three categories: 35.9% of the Arabs in 2019 (unchanged from 31.6% in 2017) chose Israeli Arab identities without a Palestinian component (Israeli, Arab, Arab in Israel, Israeli Arab), 47.1% (increase from 38.4%) chose Palestinian88 Still Playing by the Rules: Index of Arab-Jewish Relations in Israel 2019 identities with an Israeli component (Israeli Palestinian, Palestinian in Israel, Palestinian Arab in Israel), and 14.8% (down from 21.9%) chose Palestinian identities without an Israeli component (Palestinian, Palestinian-Arab). The identity of 83.0% of the Arabs in 2019 (up from 75.5% in 2017) has an Israeli component and 61.9% (unchanged from 60.3%) has a Palestinian component. However, when these two components were presented as competitors, 69.0% of the Arabs in 2019 chose exclusive or primary Palestinian identity, compared with 29.8% who chose exclusive or primary Israeli Arab identity.

University of Michigan Press. p. 13. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-472-11124-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-472-11124-4). The indigenous Palestinians comprise 20 percent of the total population of Israel. While they were allowed to become citizens, they were distanced from the center of power because the Israeli state was a *Jewish* state and Israeli national identity incorporated Jewish symbols and referents. Government officials categorized and labeled them by religion (Muslims, Christians, Druze), region (Galilee Arab, Triangle Arab, Negev Bedouin), and family connections, or *hamula* (Haberer 1985, 145). In official and popular culture, they ceased being Palestinians and were re-created as Israeli Arabs or Arab citizens of Israel. Expressing Palestinian identity by displaying the flag, singing nationalist songs, or reciting nationalist poetry was illegal in "Israel" until only very recently. Self-identification as Palestinians, Israeli Palestinians, or Palestinian citizens of Israel has increased since 1967 and is now their preferred descriptor. It was only under the influence of the *intifada*, however, that many Israeli Palestinians felt secure enough to begin to refer to themselves publicly this way (as opposed to choosing the label *Palestinian* only in anonymous surveys on identity).

Identifying the Arab minority as Palestinian has now become common practice in academic literature. This is because most Israeli citizens of Arab origin increasingly identify themselves as Palestinian, and most Arab NGOs and political parties in Israel use the label "Palestinian" to describe the identity of the Arab minority. My use of the term "Palestinian is in accordance with the [self-identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-identification) of the majority of the Arab community in Israel.

Arab-Israeli—the official media and Israeli government term for the 20 percent of Israel's almost 9 million citizens who are Arab-Palestinian—is increasingly unpopular among the people it's meant to describe. Only 16 percent of this population wants to be called "Israeli Arab," according to a 2017 survey by the University of Haifa professor Sammy Smooha provided to Foreign Policy. "The largest now and the most growing identity is a hybrid identity, which is 'Palestinian in Israel'" or a similar combination, Smooha said. "I think that's what's going to take over."

The Palestinians were included in the first population census in 1949 and were given the right to vote and be elected in the Knesset ... This notwithstanding, Israel also subjected them to a host of dominating practices. One was a discursive move involving the state's introduction of a new label to denote them: the hyphenated construct "Israeli Arabs" ('Aravim-Yisraelim) or, sometimes "Arabs of Israel" ('Arviyey-Yisrael).\
    &#x20;The new idiom Israeli Arabs, while purporting to be no more than technical, bureaucratic label, evidenced a deliberate design. A clear reflection of the politics of culture via language, it intentionally misrecognized the group's affinity with and linkage to Palestine as a territorial unit, thus facilitating the erasure of the term *Palestine* from the Hebrew vocabulary. The term puts "Israel" in the fore, constructing it as a defining feature of "its" Arabs. The Palestinians, already uprooted in the physical sense of the word, were also transformed into a group bereft of history.
